CODEX_524PASS2_20260911

I read only the four permitted files, directly from the working tree. Both edited calls are present. I did not inspect HEAD, list directories, write files, or run the detector.

1. **The mask and literal are correct for `uint32_t addr`.**

   `addr & 0xfffffffc` clears exactly bits 1 and 0 and preserves every other address bit. On ordinary implementations with 32-bit `int`, the unsuffixed hexadecimal literal has type `unsigned int`: its value does not fit signed `int`. The result remains unsigned and widens correctly when passed to `memory_rw`�?Ts `uint64_t vaddr` parameter. A wider host pointer does not change this.

   `addr & ~(uint32_t)3` produces the same result. On ordinary two�?Ts-complement implementations, `addr & ~3` does too: with 32-bit `int`, `~3` is signed `-4`, which the usual arithmetic conversions convert to the appropriate unsigned mask. The explicitly unsigned form avoids relying on signed-complement details.

   **This file�?Ts idiom is mixed.** [SWP�?Ts data-address rounding](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm_instr.c:1497) uses `addr &= ~(uint32_t)3`; the same `arm_pop` helper uses `& 0xfffffffc` when assigning PC at line 1774. The closest *data-access* precedent favors `~(uint32_t)3`, but your literal is not a correctness defect.

   Also, the generator�?Ts `0xffc` is a **page-offset mask**, applied after selecting the page. It would be wrong to substitute that narrower mask into the `memory_rw` argument.

   **The architectural qualification is substantive:** [LDM(1), A4-37](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/_scratchpad/ddi0100i.txt:7952) conditions rounding on CP15 register 1�?Ts `U == 0`, and still requires an alignment exception when alignment checking is enabled. `U == 1` requires an alignment fault for unaligned LDM. [STM(1), A4-190](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/_scratchpad/ddi0100i.txt:14113) says the same. This CP15 `U` is distinct from the instruction�?Ts increment/decrement `u_bit`.

   Neither helper checks those controls before accessing memory. Consequently, the mask implements the non-faulting word-rounding behavior; it does not establish correctness in alignment-fault modes. The permitted files do not establish whether those modes are rejected elsewhere or whether masking suppresses a check in the ARM translation callback.

2. **There are exactly two direct `memory_rw` sites in these generic BDT helpers�?"but they are not the only implementations of BDT accesses.**

   The edited sites are [LDM at 1673](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm_instr.c:1673) and [STM at 1867](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm_instr.c:1867). The ARM BDT wrappers call these helpers. The generator emits aligned host-word accesses and falls back to those wrappers.

   Your count of seven remaining calls is correct. Their classification needs correction:

   | Remaining calls in `cpu_arm_instr.c` | Operation | Address handling |
   |---|---|---|
   | 1498, 1521 | SWP read/write | `addr` already word-rounded at 1497 |
   | 1540, 1547 | SWPB read/write | One-byte accesses; low address bits select the byte |
   | 1982 | LDREX | Unaligned addresses rejected before the call |
   | 2042 | STREX | Unaligned addresses rejected before the call |
   | 4009 | Instruction fetch | Address aligned before fetching |

   These are not seven ordinary generated single-register load/store calls. Nevertheless, none is a missed generic LDM/STM call.

   **The broader completeness claim fails at two other locations:**

   - [The `netbsd_memset` combined handler](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm_instr.c:2093) replaces STM instructions but performs `memset(page + (addr & 0xfff), 0, 128)` at line 2127. Its runtime guards check page bounds, zero source values, and page availability; they do not reject an unaligned address.
   - [The `netbsd_memcpy` combined handler](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm_instr.c:2274) replaces LDM/STM instructions but copies through raw `& 0xfff` source and destination offsets. Its register-publication reads use `& 0xffc`, so the rounded register values and the byte-copy operation themselves use different address rules.

   Their [matchers](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm_instr.c:3428) recognize instruction patterns without establishing address alignment. The combined bodies bypass both edited calls. The memcpy comment at line 2252 even acknowledges a pre-existing unaligned-copy divergence.

   These defects predate your two hunks. They matter directly to �?oONLY two sites on the block-data-transfer path�??: **yes for the generic helpers�?T `memory_rw` calls; no for all implementations of the BDT access rule.**

3. **Thumb callers that actually enter these helpers automatically receive the access fix. Complete Thumb coverage is unverified.**

   Every transferred register in either helper goes through the same aligned host-word access or the newly masked slow call. There is no Thumb-specific raw-address branch inside either transfer loop. This remains true for mixed warm/cold pages.

   However, the permitted file contains comments saying the helpers are shared with Thumb; it does not contain the Thumb dispatch implementation. `cpu_arm.c` is outside your permitted set. Therefore, I cannot verify:

   - The actual PUSH/POP call sites and arguments.
   - Whether either instruction has another access path.
   - Whether Thumb LDMIA/STMIA use these helpers.
   - The reported absence of Thumb probes.

   There is also a separate reason to avoid equating shared-helper access coverage with complete Thumb POP correctness: [the helper�?Ts PC handling](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm_instr.c:1772) clears both low PC bits and calls the ARM pointer-update routine. [Thumb POP�?Ts specification](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/_scratchpad/ddi0100i.txt:24256) preserves the halfword address and specifies interworking behavior. An external Thumb caller might compensate; I cannot establish that here. This handling is unchanged by your patch.

4. **For an aligned initial base, the two argument changes are strict no-ops.**

   All running-address adjustments are multiples of four. Thus an initially aligned `addr` stays aligned, including across 32-bit wraparound. At every edited call, the old and new address arguments are identical. That includes aligned MMIO accesses.

   **The helpers are not restricted to RAM**, however. An unaligned address can reach device dispatch, and rounding can change a device-relative offset or even the selected mapping near a device boundary. Device dispatch uses the translated address before the RAM-copy path. See [device selection and offset calculation](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/memory_rw.c:480).

   The raw low bits also remain meaningful for writeback and alignment-fault detection. Moreover, [A2-42](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/_scratchpad/ddi0100i.txt:3256) specifies additional **UNPREDICTABLE** behavior for ARMv6 unaligned multiword accesses to Device or Strongly Ordered memory. The unconditional claim that all callers simply ignore those bits is too broad.

5. **Yes: the old call can touch�?"and fault on�?"a page that the corrected call never accesses.**

   First, your edit rounds the **virtual address**, before translation. [The current `memory_rw` wrapper](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/memory_rw.c:183) splits translated accesses at **1 KB** boundaries using `MRW_SPLIT_MASK == 0x3ff`. Its RAM worker separately uses a 4 KB offset mask for ARM and performs byte copies.

   For a word-aligned physical address, the worker�?Ts arithmetic is appropriate: the maximum 4 KB page offset is `0xffc`, and adding four reaches the page�?Ts exclusive end without crossing it. The same reasoning applies to the wrapper�?Ts 1 KB granule.

   Consider a four-byte access starting at `B - 1`, where `B` is a page boundary:

   | Version | Bytes requested | Translation behavior |
   |---|---|---|
   | Old | `B - 1` through `B + 2` | Can access both mappings |
   | New | `B - 4` through `B - 1` | Accesses only the preceding mapping |

   If the preceding mapping succeeds and the next mapping faults, the old slow call can fail while the new call succeeds. Old stores can also perform a partial first-chunk write before the second chunk fails.

   **That is an observable fault change, but it is part of correcting the erroneous byte range** in the non-faulting word-rounding regime. Preserving that second-page access would preserve the defect.

   A multiple-register instruction can still legitimately reach another page on a subsequent aligned word. Rounding does not remove that access.

   Translation callbacks, write-watchpoints, and device callbacks also receive the rounded address. I cannot certify unchanged ARM fault-address-register contents: the callback implementing ARM translation and fault reporting is outside the permitted files.

6. **The entry-mask mutant tests writeback preservation; it does not null the access-rounding effect.**

   It still rounds every access correctly. Its defect is that writeback loses the original low bits. It is useful, but it exercises a different invariant from �?othe mask remains syntactically present while its effect disappears.�??

   A closer clause-(d) mutant is an **identity-mask mutant**: retain the calls and their failure checks, but use `addr & 0xffffffff` at both edited sites. For this `uint32_t`, the alignment effect disappears completely. Its address arguments reproduce the original behavior. Any score prediction would still be an inference from your reported HEAD result, not a new measurement.

   The smaller partial mutants have these detection requirements:

   | Mutant | Required failing observation |
   |---|---|
   | Leave only LDM fixed | Cold, unaligned STM memory-result assertion |
   | Leave only STM fixed | Cold, unaligned LDM loaded-value assertion |
   | Use `& 0xfffffffe` | Cold access with address low bits `10` or `11`, checking distinguishing data |

   Half-masking correctly handles low bits `00` and `01`; it leaves `10` and `11` at offset two. It preserves raw writeback. Thus aligned rows, writeback-only rows, and unaligned rows using only offset `+1` can all miss it. Warm helper accesses also bypass the mutated slow call.

   **I cannot name an actual shipped row that turns red.** Its names, addresses, fixtures, and assertions are absent from the permitted files. Your reported `21/25` results say the one-site mutants were caught, but do not identify their failing rows or establish coverage of half-masking.

7. **The comment�?Ts core explanation is supported; its scope and measurement claims need different treatment.**

   The source supports access-only rounding, preservation of the running address for writeback, and the generator precedent. [The generator](C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/generate_arm_multi.c:117) masks its page offset while emitting writeback separately from the original register value.

   Two explanatory statements should be tightened:

   - Qualify the architectural rule with the alignment-control conditions discussed above.
   - Describe the **RAM worker** as copying bytes without word rounding. `memory_rw` also splits transfers and dispatches devices; it is not universally one raw `memcpy`.

   **I could not verify the `20/25` or `22/22` scores.** Their appearance in the comment confirms what the comment claims, not that the measurements occurred. /*
 *  arm_cpu_interpret_thumb_SLOW():
 *
 *  Slow interpretation of THUMB instructions.
 *
 *  TODO: Either replace this with dyntrans or
 *  implement ARM (including THUMB) in the new framework. :-)
 *  For now, this is better than nothing.
 */
int arm_cpu_interpret_thumb_SLOW(struct cpu *cpu)
{
	uint16_t iw;
	uint8_t ib[sizeof(uint16_t)];
	uint32_t addr = cpu->pc & ~1;

	if (!(cpu->pc & 1) || !(cpu->cd.arm.cpsr & ARM_FLAG_T)) {
		fatal("arm_cpu_interpret_thumb_SLOW called when not in "
			"THUMB mode?\n");
		cpu->running = 0;
		return 0;
	}

	if (!cpu->memory_rw(cpu, cpu->mem, addr, &ib[0],
			sizeof(ib), MEM_READ, CACHE_INSTRUCTION)) {
		fatal("arm_cpu_interpret_thumb_SLOW(): could not read "
			"the instruction\n");
		cpu->running = 0;
		return 0;
	}
	
	if (cpu->machine->instruction_trace) {
		uint64_t offset;
		char* symbol = get_symbol_name(&cpu->machine->symbol_context,
		    addr, &offset);
		if (symbol != NULL && offset == 0)
			debug("<%s>\n", symbol);

		if (cpu->machine->ncpus > 1)
			debug("cpu%i:\t", cpu->cpu_id);

		debug("%08x:  ", (int)addr & ~1);
		arm_cpu_disassemble_instr_thumb(cpu, ib, 1, cpu->pc);
	}

	if (cpu->byte_order == EMUL_LITTLE_ENDIAN)
		iw = ib[0] + (ib[1]<<8);
	else
		iw = ib[1] + (ib[0]<<8);

	cpu->ninstrs ++;

	int main_opcode = (iw >> 12) & 15;
	int condition_code = (iw >> 8) & 15;
	int op10 = (iw >> 10) & 3;
	int op11_8 = (iw >> 8) & 15;
	int rd8 = (iw >> 8) & 7;
	int r6 = (iw >> 6) & 7;
	int r3 = (iw >> 3) & 7;
	int rd = (iw >> 0) & 7;
	int hd = (iw >> 7) & 1;
	int hm = (iw >> 6) & 1;
			debug("TODO: unimplemented opcode 0x%x, non-branch, at pc 0x%08x\n", main_opcode, (int)cpu->pc);
			cpu->running = 0;
			return 0;
		}
		break;

	case 0xe:
		if (iw & 0x0800) {
			// blx
			addr = (cpu->pc + 4 + (cpu->cd.arm.tmp_branch + (((iw >> 1) & 0x3ff) << 2))) & ~3;

			if (iw & 1) {
				fatal("lowest bit set in thumb blx instruction?\n");
				cpu->running = 0;
				return 0;
			}

			cpu->cd.arm.r[ARM_LR] = cpu->pc + 2;
			cpu->pc = addr;
			if (cpu->machine->show_trace_tree)
				cpu_functioncall_trace(cpu, cpu->pc);
			cpu->cd.arm.cpsr &= ~ARM_FLAG_T;
			return 1;
		} else {
			// b
			tmp = (iw & 0x7ff) << 1;
			if (tmp & 0x800)
				tmp |= 0xfffff000;
			cpu->pc = (int32_t)(cpu->pc + 4 + tmp);
			return 1;
		}
		break;

	case 0xf:
		if (iw & 0x0800) {
			// bl
			addr = (cpu->pc + 2 + (cpu->cd.arm.tmp_branch + ((iw & 0x7ff) << 1)));
			cpu->cd.arm.r[ARM_LR] = cpu->pc + 2;
			cpu->pc = addr;
			if (cpu->machine->show_trace_tree)
				cpu_functioncall_trace(cpu, cpu->pc);

codex
The earlier Thumb finding is wrong as stated. After `arm_pop()` returns, the Thumb caller restores `cpu->pc` from the loaded R15 and sets T from bit 0. Its next fetch clears only bit 0, so the helper’s temporary word rounding does not establish the claimed loss of the halfword address.

The ARM mask still needs the A/U qualification. I have not settled whether SA1110’s MMU-disabled configuration makes the detector’s enabled A bit effective, so I won’t claim a required fault there.

NOT RATIFIED — both detectors have concrete escapes, and the earlier Thumb-PC finding is wrong as stated.

I read the **uncommitted working tree under `GXEMUL-SEC/src`**, plus both detector scripts. I ran no gates, battery, detectors, or mutants. Escape predictions below are source deductions, not new measurements.

1. **The smallest escapes I can substantiate are:**

   | Round | Edit | Why existing rows miss it | Distinguishing case |
   |---|---|---|---|
   | `#524` | Change either access mask’s final `c` to `e`: `0xfffffffe` | Every unaligned transfer has low bits `01`; clearing bit 0 produces exactly the existing expected addresses. | Cold access with low bits `10` or `11`. |
   | `#525` | Replace `odata = 0` with `odata = d->drive` | Every row selects drive 0. | Offset-1 read with drive 1 selected returns 1. |

   An explicitly conditioned `#525` repair, `if (!d->drive) odata = 0;`, likewise escapes while restoring the original status-byte defect for drive 1. Conditioning on `!d->base_drive` misses the secondary controller instead.

   `#524` also permits a **register selector** escape: apply the mask only when `i == 1`. Every data assertion concerns transfers of R1; the two-register LDM case checks only base writeback. The relevant evidence is in the [ARM detector](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/regress/artifacts/armbdt/agy_armbdt_witness_20260911.py:189) and [WDC detector](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/regress/artifacts/wdcaltdigin/agy_altdigin_witness_20260911.py:96).

2. **The earlier Thumb correction overstates the failure.** After `arm_pop()` returns, the Thumb caller assigns `cpu->pc = cpu->cd.arm.r[ARM_PC]` and derives T from bit 0. The Thumb fetch then clears **only bit 0**. Thus the helper’s temporary `& 0xfffffffc` does not establish the claimed final loss of bit 1. See the [caller](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm.c:1675) and [fetch](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/cpu_arm.c:1210). This refutes that mechanism; it does not certify every interworking case.

3. **I have not demonstrated an introduced functional regression in either fix.** However, `#524`’s unconditional architectural justification is false. The rounding rule requires the appropriate non-faulting configuration: alignment checking can require a fault, and ARMv6 CP15 U=1 requires alignment faults for unaligned multiple transfers. CP15 U is distinct from the instruction’s `u_bit`. [DDI0100I, A4-37 and A4-190](https://documentation-service.arm.com/static/5f8dacc8f86e16515cdb865a)

   Neither helper nor the ARM translation callbacks implement that alignment check. Consequently, I found no existing alignment check that these masks newly bypass. Missing required faults are a separate correctness defect.

   The constructor sets A, and the detector never changes it. I have **not settled SA1110’s MMU-disabled alignment behavior**, so I would not assert that these particular runs must fault solely from that observation.

4. **The mask is valid C99, and its position is right for selecting the transferred bytes.** `addr` is `uint32_t`; `0xfffffffc` clears exactly its bottom two bits without changing writeback.

   Successful ARM translations preserve the low address bits, so numerically:

   ```text
   translate(addr & ~3) == translate(addr) & ~3
   ```

   But moving rounding into the translated worker is worse: [the wrapper splits first](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/memory_rw.c:185). A four-byte request at `0x203ff` becomes chunks spanning `0x20400`; the intended rounded word occupies `0x203fc..0x203ff`. Rounding individual chunks afterward cannot repair that decomposition.

   Finding 6 therefore describes removal of an erroneous access, not a reason to preserve it. Required alignment checks must nevertheless inspect the original address before rounding.

   One remaining qualification: [translation faults publish their input VA into FAR](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/memory_arm.c:271). The patch changes FAR’s low bits for faults that still occur. I have not settled the required per-core reporting there.

5. **Device dispatch is a scope disclosure, not itself a counterexample.** An emulator device callback is not synonymous with an ARM **Device memory attribute**. Where rounding is specified, the callback should receive the resulting access. ARMv6’s Device/Strongly Ordered exception makes certain otherwise normal unaligned accesses UNPREDICTABLE; it does **not** override mandatory alignment faults. That distinction prevents using UNPREDICTABLE as blanket justification. [DDI0100I, A2-42](https://documentation-service.arm.com/static/5f8dacc8f86e16515cdb865a)

6. **For `#525`’s chosen handled-and-declined policy, I rank the alternatives:**

   | Rank | Response | Consequence |
   |---|---|---|
   | 1 | Explicit zero | Deterministic and explicit. |
   | 2 | Leave buffer untouched, skipping the common assignment | Equivalent through current dispatch because [#95 pre-zeroes it](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/cpus/memory_rw.c:485); depends on that caller contract. |
   | 3 | `0xff` | Requires an independently justified bus/register convention. |
   | 4 | Fault | Introduces guest exceptions for an address deliberately retained as handled. |
   | 5 | Last-written value | Invents storage semantics for a register whose writes are ignored. |

   This is a **policy ranking**, not an established hardware ranking. Zero does not implement Drive Address semantics, and returning 1 does not hand the access to a floppy controller.

   I verified the complete status computation: [status_byte()](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/devices/dev_wdc.c:584) calls only [diskimage_exist()](/C:/DocumentNoSnc/CC/GXEMUL/GXEMUL-SEC/src/disk/diskimage.c:111), which merely walks a list. Neither mutates device state or acknowledges interrupts.

7. **Use two commits.** They have independent semantics, validation, and revert decisions.

The next measurements I would request are cold ARM transfers at offsets `+2/+3`, a transfer using R2 with data assertions, a mapped boundary with the following mapping absent, and WDC offset-1 reads on drive 1 and the secondary channel with nonzero status. Also test interrupt preservation before reading main status: both existing WDC phases read main status first, and no row observes the interrupt line.
